### Post #774: 2025-12-06 23:54

**Put Options: Why do they Exist?**

A put option is a financial contract that gives the buyer the right, but not the obligation, to sell a specific quantity of an underlying asset (such as a stock) at a predetermined price (the strike price) within a specified period. The seller of the put option is obligated to buy the underlying asset at the strike price if the buyer exercises the option. __Most of us should be put sellers__.

The graph below represents the risk profile of a put option at the chosen expiration date.
As the price of the stock decreases, the value of the options contract you hold will increase. There is no limit to how much the option will increase in value. However as the stock price increases, the value of the option will decrease, but only to a maximum of the premium you paid to buy that put option.

**How Do Put Options Work?**
When a trader buys a put option, they pay a premium to the seller for the right to sell the underlying asset at the strike price. If the market price of the asset falls below the strike price before the option expires, the buyer can exercise the option to sell stock at the put option strike, even though the market price is lower than their strike. If the stock price does not fall below the strike price, the option expires worthless, and the buyer loses only the premium paid. This basically acts as insurance for the shareholder if they want to protect their gains if the stock falls.

__For instance:__
- A trader fears that Stock ABC will move lower from the current price of $25 and they own 100 shares at a cost basis of $20.
- They purchase a put option for Stock ABC at a strike price of $25 with a one week expiration of 6/14. This put option contract costed them $175.
- On 6/12, Stock ABC closes at a price of $23. The trader executes the contract and sells their 100 shares at the strike price of $25. The net profit from this trade is ($25 - $20) x 100 shares - $175 premium = $325.

However, most traders will not exercise put options as described above. As the price of the underlying stock moves lower, the value of your put option will increase. You can then sell your put option contract and your profit will be the difference between the premium you paid and the premium at the time of the sale. This is basically another way of shorting a stock.

__Using the same example as above:__
- A trader believes Stock ABC will move lower from the current price of $25, and they don't own any shares.
- Instead of shorting the shares, they purchase a put option for Stock ABC at a strike price of $25 with a one week expiry on 6/21. This put option contract costed them $175.
- On 6/12, Stock ABC close at a price of $23. The option contract they are holding is now worth $575 and they choose to sell it. The net profit from this trade is $575 - $175 = $400.

*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/bb8c/put-options-why-do-they-exist*

### Post #780: 2025-12-09 17:29

**Trading Psychology: Overtrading**

Overtrading is one of the most common and destructive behaviors in trading. It happens because the human brain is wired in ways that directly conflict with successful market behavior. In this short article, I'll share 2 common reasons for overtrading and 3 things you can do to stop it.

__Two Most Common Reasons for Overtrading__
- The Need for Constant Action: When you’re sitting in front of charts for hours, it feels like more activity should lead to better results. In reality, markets reward selectivity and patience, not effort. The harder you try to “make something happen,” the more likely you are to force trades.
- FOMO and Market Noise: There’s always a stock moving somewhere, and social media amplifies that pressure. When you see others posting wins, it triggers a fear of missing out — and FOMO leads to chasing, late entries, and oversized position frequency.

__Three Ways to Stop Overtrading__
- Define Your A+ Setups and Trade Nothing Else: When your criteria are crystal clear, anything outside of that is automatically a “no."
- Track Why You Entered the Trade: Log the reason for every entry. Your reasons should always be based on your best trade setups, never because you were bored or had FOMO.
- Learn How to Tolerate Boredom: Successful traders tolerate boredom exceptionally well. They understand that *waiting* is a position.

Remember, the best traders aren’t the most active. They’re the most selective.

*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/bcjc/trading-psychology-overtrading*

- Fed will purchase short-term treasuries to keep effective control of policy rate - and keeping an ample supply of reserves over time. $40bn in the first month is planned.
- Median GDP projections are somewhat stronger than they were back in September. GDP projections have increased, signaling stronger than expected economy moving forward.
- Both layoffs and hiring remains low. Soft labor market conditions are expected to improve next year.
- 4.5% projection by end of this year for unemployment, at which point will edge down next year
- Disinflation continues for services. PCE inflation median projection is 2% by 2027 and near 2% in 2026.
- 3 rate cuts are planned for next year to bring rates down to 3.1% with the longer-term goal being 3% 2027 and after.
- 9 out of 12 members supported this rate cut. Only 3 dissents, that were thoughtful and good discussions. 
- Fed is still neutral and in wait-and-see mode. Tension between inflation and employment risks forces this stance.

**December 2025 FOMC - Q&A - Part 1:**
- What's driving optimism? Pickup in growth and consumer spending has held up. AI spending and data centers has been holding up business investment. Fiscal policy will be supportive of growth as well.
- More growth but unemployment unchanged and even slightly worsening? Productivity has been structurally higher due to AI and technology. 
- __A rate hike is nobody's base case at this point__. 
- They haven't seen any evidence of a potential spike up in unemployment. They are choosing target rates that will keep employment stable and allow inflation to continue cooling.
- Why did you cut today? Unemployment is weakening, declining supply and demand for workers. Labor market has continued to cool gradually (just a touch more than they thought). Inflation has been coming in a touch lower than they thought. This doesn't feel like a hot economy where inflation is at risk with looser conditions.
- How long will tariff inflation take to pass through? They should peak in the first quarter of next year and come back down in the second half of next year.
- Elevated yields on long-term bonds are factored by inflation-compensation. There are still concerns of long-term inflation.
- Fed's policy stance also wants to support real wages going up by supporting the labor market so that people can earn more money and afford things again.

**December 2025 FOMC - Q&A - Part 2:**
- Why is job growth expected to worse than what the data shows? It's difficult to estimate job growth in real-time and there are systematic overcounts. They estimate 60k less jobs than reported on each report.
- AI is a small factor in the labor market weakening. They see the announcement of layoffs citing AI, but they don't see people filing for unemployment insurance. If there were big layoffs, you would see higher amount of unemployment benefits being filed. Powell said it's curious we're not seeing that 👀 
- How sustainable is a K-Shaped Economy (current state)? Not sure, but the best the Feds can do is have price stability and a stable labor market. However, most of economic consumption is impacted by people in the higher income levels.
- Housing market is facing significant challenges. Housing supply is low and many people have low-rate mortgages from COVID which made it expensive for them to move. We are far away from that changing. Need to build more housing, but FOMC can't do much to impact the housing market except for interest rate adjustments.


*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/bcp9/december-2025-fomc-summary-of-*

**$HIMS Chart Update - Why I'm Bearish**

Don't get me wrong, $HIMS has solid fundamentals - low debt, positive free cash flow, double digits EPS 3-5 yr growth projection. But as a trade, this looks like it's heading lower to me. Here's why:

- The 50 SMA (blue) crossed below the 150 SMA (green) and are both sloping down. If you've been following me, then you know this is one of my favorite indications for long-term trends. What $HIMS is showing is a __long-term downtrend.__
- Price broke out of the previous trading range it was in between $40 and $66. I knew that we would get a bounce where buyers would try to recover that. The bounce failed to break back into the range at $40 and has since dropped back down to $35. That is another bearish signal.

I know when to recognize a trade hasn't worked out, and I'm willing to take the loss on it and look for better opportunities. If $HIMS gets down to $25, I'll re-evaluate and maybe even buy back in. If $HIMS rallies back into the range after I sell, then great - I'll also go back to trading it.

This post isn't only an update for $HIMS. I'm sharing how I handle and process a loss, as a profitable trader, so that you can adjust and become one too.

### Post #805: 2025-12-29 19:44 — `$SPY`

**Market Study: Mid-Term Year Seasonality**

I performed a study of the $SPY performance over the last 12 mid-term election years (1978 - 2022). The years highlighted in yellow represent times where the market started the year significantly below the ATH (usually due to a recovery from a bear market).

These are the results:
- The most bearish months are January, April, June, and September - where both averages are significantly red.
- The most bullish months are March, July, October, and November - where both averages are significantly green.
- The remaining months tend to have mixed results based on which average you look at. These months are considered to be uncertain.

__How Do I Use this to my Advantage?__
It's important to note that this is not an exact science and the market can not follow seasonality. This only shows what the market has historically done each month. Prioritize your market analysis before taking this into account. Having said that, here is how I'd use this info (if my analysis also lines up with the seasonality):
- De-risk and build cash positions ahead of the bearish months and get ready to buy stock for the long-term and sell cash-secured puts. 
- Be more aggressive with your trading during the bullish months.

*As an investor and trader, it's not your job to predict what the markets will do - but rather prepare for any likely scenario to happen so you can take advantage of the opportunities presented to you.*

*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/bdGH/market-study-mid-term-year-sea*

### Post #829: 2026-01-19 00:22 — `$JNJ`

**8 Warren Buffett Quotes Every Invest Needs to Know**

Edit: The should say Investor instead of invest lol

Warren Buffett’s words have shaped generations of investors because they cut through market noise and focus on what truly matters. Rather than short-term speculation, his quotes emphasize discipline, patience, and owning great businesses. In this post, I’ll highlight the most important Warren Buffett quotes every investor should know—and why they still matter today.

Here's a list of my favorite ones that I apply to my investment decisions:

1/ **"I'd rather buy a wonderful business at a fair price, than a fair business at a wonderful price." "Time is the friend of the wonderful business - you keep compounding and keep making more money. Time is the enemy of a lousy company."**
- Buffett was talking about his "cigar butt" investing approach where he would find very cheap businesses that had "one puff" left in them and sell them for a profit. He later realized that this was a mistake and it was much better to buy  wonderful businesses where you investment can continue compounding over a long period of time. For instance, Berkshire Hathaway's investment into Apple in 2016 and returned 587% as of April 2025. I personally have made successful investment decisions by following this wisdom by buying Costco while it was slightly over-valued in 2023 (which has returned me 53% since then) and by buying $JNJ while it was fair-valued in August 2025 (which has returned me 25% so far).

—//—

2/ **"What I'm going to buy is the business where I feel extremely certain about the competitive advantage, extremely certain about the integrity and ability of the management, and where I think the discounted flow of the cash that the business will produce over time makes sense in relation to its current price."**
- This is a concise and actionable list of criteria that is fundamental to consider when selecting a business to invest in. Notice the use of the word "extremely" - it must feel like a certainty. A great example is Costco: I'm almost certain about their competitive advantage and integrity of their management.

—//—

3/ **"The definition of a great company is that it will stay great for 30 years. If it's gonna be a great company for 3 years, then it ain't a great company." "The idea is that if you're going to take a trip out of the country for 20 years, you wouldn't feel bad leaving your position with no power to sell - knowing that you'll come back it's going to still be a terribly strong company." "Great businesses are too hard to find to just sit there and hope you buy them in the throws of some panic."**
- This was in response to a question during one of Berkshire Hathaway's annual meeting where someone asked if they should wait for great companies to be pounded down or just buy them now - knowing they'll hold for the next 20 years. He stated the above and said it's better just to own them.

—//—

4/ **"Being very greedy in the stock market will be a disaster for you because that will overcome rationality."**
- Greedy can manifest in feelings of FOMO, over-leveraging, poor position sizing, and choosing stocks based on their high returns and not their fundamentals.

—//—

5/ **"The most important thing in hitting is waiting for the right pitch." "People can throw Microsoft at me, any stock - General Motors, and I don't have to swing. I only get a strike called if I swing at a pitch and miss. So I cant wait there and look at thousands of companies, day after day, and only when I see something I understand and when I like the price at which it's selling, then I can swing."**
- In baseball if you don't swing at 3 pitches in the batter's box, then you're out. However in investing, the market can pitch all kinds of stocks to you and you don't have to swing because there's no strike limit. The point he is making here is that it's important to be selective and to only "take a swing" at a stock when the pitch comes in at the sweet spot - and that sweet spot is when you understand the business and you like the price it's selling at.

—//—

6/ **"You want something where price competition is not going to be the determining factor of that business's success."**
- This means that when a business (let's say Samsung) sells a cheaper but identical version of the product of another business (Apple iPhone) - the majority of Apple customers (generally) will still prefer to buy Apple's smartphones over Samsung's. Even if the price is cheaper. That reflects the quality of Apple's economic moat around their business and shows how they are not easily disrupted. People choose Apple products because of something more than just price. That's a business you want to invest in.

—//—

7/ **"It's a terrible mistake to think that you have to have an opinion on everything. You only have to have an opinion on a few things. I told students that when they got out of school, they get a punch-card with 20 punches on it - and that's all the investing decisions they get to make in their entire lives, then they'd be very rich because they would think very hard about each one. And you don't need 20 right decisions to get very rich. 4 or 5 will probably do it."**
- This touches on the fact that you don't need to own every great company to get rich in stocks. You only need to invest in the best of the best companies. Your job is to figure out what those companies are, and then which of those do you understand the most and believe is selling at a fair price. Luckily you're here and I can help you with that.

—//—

8/ **"The ideal business is one that earns very high returns on capital and keep using lots of capital at those high returns - that becomes a compounding machine."**
- This is self-explanatory, but in essence a company that is able to achieve a high ROIC means they are really good making money from the capital it uses. This also ties in with the previous quote about time being the friend of a wonderful businesses. The more time passes - the stronger the returns from that compounding.

*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/bfwG/8-warren-buffett-quotes-every-*

### Post #840: 2026-01-28 00:19 — `$JNJ`

**Buffett Quotes Every Investor Must Apply #1**

As we build our cash positions to invest during the next market pullback, I wanted to post my favorite Buffett quotes individually with some of my thoughts. 

Here is the first quote I want to share:

**"I'd rather buy a wonderful business at a fair price, than a fair business at a wonderful price." "Time is the friend of the wonderful business - you keep compounding and keep making more money. Time is the enemy of a lousy company."**

- Buffett was talking about his "cigar butt" investing approach where he would find very cheap businesses that had "one puff" left in them and sell them for a profit. He later realized that this was a mistake and it was much better to buy wonderful businesses where you investment can continue compounding over a long period of time. 

- For instance, Berkshire Hathaway's investment into Apple in 2016 and returned 587% as of April 2025. I personally have made successful investment decisions by following this wisdom by buying Costco while it was slightly over-valued in 2023 (which has returned me 53% since then) and by buying $JNJ while it was fair-valued in August 2025 (which has returned me 25% so far)

### Post #843: 2026-01-28 23:38

**FOMC Notes - January 28, 2026**


- __Rates held at current rates of 3.5-3.75.__

- Consumer spending is resilient and economy continues to expand at a solid rate. 
- Gov shutdown slowing effects from last quarter are expected to reverse this quarter.
- Unemployment rate remains mostly unchanged, while the job gains have remained low due to lower immigration and labor force participation.
- The outlook for economic activity has improved since the last meeting, therefore the labor market is expected to continue to stabilize as the affects of inflation from tariffs pass through.
- Current rates are at the higher end of the long-term neutral range. Fed believes they are well-positioned currently to allow economic data to lead the way on whether they cut more or not.
- Most of the current overrun on prices is from tariffs. Current economy is not hot enough to raise inflation - therefore there is an expectation for the tariff effects to peak and pass through over the course of this year.__ If we see that, then we can loosen policy.__
- It's not in anybody's base case that the next move is a rate hike.
- Labor market data is more reliable than GDP data. Much better to look at 12 month GDP vs quarterly GDP.
- It is NOT the case that the surge in precious metals means that the US is losing credibility.

**My Biggest Takeaways:**
- The Fed believes the downside economic risks have diminished, but are still there.
- If tariff inflation passes through the economy over the course of this year - then more rate cuts are possible.

This means to keep an eye on the inflation reports every month to ensure they come in at or below expectations!

### Post #859: 2026-02-12 01:54

**My Top Trading Lessons from 2025**

The key to success in the markets is to look back on your trades and identify weak points and patterns that you can improve. I hope sharing these will fast-track your journey to success!

**1/ Only sell covered calls on stocks you're willing to lose**
- I learned this with GOOG. I sold weekly covers at 0.1-0.15 delta and got called away around $200 on shares I'd accumulated at $175. I sold covered calls just to make some extra cash every week and that was the wrong mindset to have. I've missed out on big gains because of that.

**2/ Enter swing positions faster on Wyckoff setups**
- Immediately enter 50% of my position when the setup triggers, then set a limit order for 25% at the re-test level. Finally, add the final 25% when the candle closes above the prior high the next day. This ensures you get a favorable basis while minimizing your losses on fake-outs.

**3/ Don't overstay your welcome in winning trades**
- UBER, VEEV, and TOST all taught me this lesson. I held on too long and watched my profits evaporate. The solution? Use weekly ATR to set profit targets and set a limit order to lock in gains!

**4/ Position sizing directly impacts your ability to trade**
- APP was over 30% of my portfolio when I got assigned at $325. I ended up selling at $350, but would've made more money selling covers rather than getting spooked by volatility. If APP was a smaller weight in my port, it would've been easier to manage.

**5/ Never hold swing trades through earnings**
- The risk-reward just doesn't justify it. Period.

**6/ Wait 5-10 trading days after large gap move**
- Whether the gap is up or down, the stock needs time to establish a new pattern before trading.

**7/ When your thesis breaks, cut loss quickly**
- Don't hope and don't wait for a bounce. Set a limit order, manually sell shares, or sell covered calls to exit. HIMS was my painful reminder of this rule—when the reason you entered a trade no longer exists, the trade itself shouldn't exist either.

*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/bhG1/my-top-trading-lessons-from-20*

On the economic front, both the Manufacturing and Services PMI came in higher than expected and are both expanding. This means that both of the main components of the US economy are expanding, which shows breadth returning to the economy - which is good and healthy. Still no signs of a recession - with the exception being the yield curve un-inverting. The yield curve un-inversion is a reliable predictor of recessions, __but not perfect__. It's predicted 7 of the last 9 recessions and usually a recession will follow within 6 months of the re-inversion.

The yield curve re-inverted November 2025 (as shown by the 10Y - 3M yield curve). Theoretically, we should see a recession by May of this year - but there are no indications of that currently. The main indicator would be unemployment rate rising rapidly - so keep an eye on that.

I'm not comfortable selling CSPs here as there's a decent chance we break the 150 SMA and hit the 200 SMA next week. 

On the economic front, we got a bad jobs report which showed -92k jobs created last month vs forecasted 59k jobs. Additionally if you apply the -60k adjustment the Fed uses to account for inaccuracies in the reporting, that means we saw job losses of -152k jobs lost last month. This also caused unemployment to tick up to 4.4%.

The middle east conflict is also pushing the price of oil higher, which puts upwards pressure on inflation. The rate cut odds this year have already shifted from 2 cuts this year to only 1 - not what the market is looking for. The mid-term election year is living up to its name for being volatile so far - let's see what comes next.

### Post #894: 2026-03-18 19:23

**FOMC Notes March 2026**

**FOMC Notes**:
- Housing activity is weak. Labor demand has softened.
- GDP projected to continue growing.
- Median unemployment rate for this year is 4.4% and is projected to edge down in future years.
- Oil prices rising by supply disruptions are causing near term inflation risk.
- PCE expected to be 2.7% this year, higher than projected back in December.
- Too soon to know scope and duration of Middle East conflict on US economy at this time. Higher energy costs will put pressure on inflation to rise.
- No change in rate cut projections (one this year and one next year).

__Q&A:__
- FOMC wants to see progress on inflation in terms of consumer goods.
- The forecast is we'll be making some progress in inflation, especially from tariffs in the middle of the year. If we don't see progress, the we won't see a rate cut.
- Inflation spike projection this year, some of it is from oil shock. They haven't seen the progress they hoped for on consumer goods from tariffs. We believe we'll see the progress, but just longer than expected.
- US is net exporter of energy, so it would be offset by oil shock. However, it really depends on how persistent higher oil prices will be - not a certain price level per barrel.
- They believe inflation will hit 2% target despite economic conditions because they believe the target interest rate of 3% is still considered in the modestly to mildly restrictive. They also believe that inflation is still above target due to shocks like tariffs and oil prices, which will pass through.
- There's concern over the current low levels of job creation. Immigration policy is the biggest factor in this metric.
- Stagflation is not an accurate term for current situation since unemployment is not double digits and inflation is just 1% above target, unlike the 1970s (where the term came from).
- People feel squeezed despite wages going up, since services are also going up. It will take time as wage growth continues to feel comfortable

*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/bj5P/fomc-notes-march-2026*

### Post #908: 2026-04-01 01:15 — `$SPY`

**How to Determine if we are beginning a Bear Market or ending a Correction Using $SPY**

The 200 SMA and Swing Levels are the main technical indicators needed for this analysis. The idea is to be able to identify as fast as possible whether the market will resume its uptrend or if it’s beginning a new downtrend. 

This is what I expect to see:
- $SPY to retrace the swing low support of $653. This is based on price action fundamentals on trend analysis (like I've explained during my livestreams). If SPY breaks above it, then we can consider the uptrend may be recovered and the next target is:
- the 200 SMA (~$662). If a recession is truly on the horizon, then this re-test would fail. The down-trend would resume and the next target would be the Pre-Tariff Drop ATH at $613. I would expect another bounce there.
- If we break above the 200 SMA, then it's possible for this pullback to be over. However, false breakouts are very common before bear markets and it would be dangerous to be aggressive on that. We can reference the 2022 Bear Market for this.

Look at the price action before the 2022 bear market:
- The market bounced off the Swing low support level and the 300 SMA (red). 
- Next the market break out above the 200 SMA, only to fail.
- The market re-tests the Swing low support and bounces again.
- The 200 SMA is broken above again only for it to fail and ultiamtely:
- The market broke below the Swing Low Suppport and confirmed a downtrend

—//—

The biggest takeaway from my experience is that you will not be able to get in or get out early unless you’re lucky. It’s best to simply reduce your position sizes on trades during this time as it’s a low probability trading environment. 

This is simply to help you mark your targets where you can take action based on your analysis on whether or not we’ll be seeing a recession this year (which I think we will).

*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/bk8C/how-to-determine-if-we-are-beg*

Not much change from yesterday's analysis. $SPY has hit the downtrend resistance and so far has failed to break above it (see chart below). However, I can't deny that this bounce looks bullish and pretty convincing. I'm basing my short-term and long-term bias off my gut here by keeping them at Neutral. If I went strictly based off of the technicals (which we should be doing), then they'd both say Bullish.

I just can't move past the economic backdrop as of now. Inflation rose from 2.4% to 3.3% in one month from this oil spike! That means prices will continue to move higher in an environment where we're already feeling squeezed. 

However, it's also valid to note that inflation being at 3.3% is not devastating either. To me, it all comes down to the labor market. If layoffs continue to happen and unemployment rises, then the Feds won't have a choice but to let a recession happen before they can step in with rate cuts and stimulus. 

But if consumers can continue eating these cost increases, then I guess a recession could still be avoided. It's a very uncertain time in the economy right now, which is reflected in the market price action (trading sideways, dropping 10%, and bouncing). 

This is why it's important to stay invested in __fundamentally good companies__. If I'm wrong, at least I still have my long-term holdings that allow me to participate in the market.

### Post #926: 2026-04-22 01:05

**My Current Thoughts on the Market and Economy**

Before I start I want to make it clear that as retail investors and traders, we have to make a distinction between what our opinion is and what the market is doing. There's no way for us to be more informed than institutions and fund managers. That's because 1) it's their job to be as informed as possible so they spend all of their time gathering info and 2) because their companies spend thousands, if not millions, of dollars to get this information. For us retail, we have to invest in the best companies for the long-term and trade with the trend that we identify in the short-term.

Having said that, I personally think we will be seeing a recession this year. This is the main reason why:
- __Yield Curve Un-Inversion__: 10Y-1Y US Treasury Yield Curve Un-Inversions have predicted the past 7 recessions since the 1970s. The key is that the Yield Curve Spread becomes negative (meaning it inverted), then returns back to positive (meaning it un-inverted). Below is a screenshot of the 10Y-1Y Yield Spread from GuruFocus. The shaded areas indicate recessions. When the spread goes beyond 0.5%, then the window of recession is 0-6 months from that date. That happened in December 2025, which puts the window between December 2025 and June 2026.

Whether the reason is the oil shock, inflation spiking, unemployment rising, etc. - there is risk here. 

__What does that mean for us? __
It means that this is the time to clean out your long-term portfolio. Make sure you're only holding stocks that you know will survive a recession (utilize the stock valuation resources I have in my Discord ). It also means that you start budgeting in your personal life and make sure you have an emergency savings fund in the bank as well as saving money to deposit into your stock account to buy as prices head lower. 

As for trading, we continue trading with the trend until a bear market is confirmed.

*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/bmIx/my-current-thoughts-on-the-mar*

### Post #933: 2026-04-29 19:45

**FOMC Notes April 2026**

- High uncertainty to economic outlook due to Middle East conflict.
- Interest rates held at 3.5-3.75%.
- This is Powell's last press conference as chairman.
- Besides inflation, little change to economic measures (employment = stagnant, consumer spending = resilient, and housing = weak).

__Q&A:__
- There's concern from the legal actions taken by administration on the Federal Reserve and how it affects the Fed's ability to make monetary decisions without getting pulled into politics.
- How has the inflation outlooked changed due to the current energy shock? We expect the inflation shock from tariffs to subside now (within the next quarter or two) - __we need to see that now__. Feds believe that the energy shock has not peaked yet and they're not considering to lower rates until they see the backend of this shock and until they see the tariff inflation subside. 
- Feds aren't thinking "should we hike rates now" - they're thinking "we should remain neutral." They're in a good stance to wait and see. Monetary policy is on the high end of neutral, and this could be the right place to be to wait, let things develop, and react accordingly.
- Powell will go back to being a Fed governor in the FOMC after stepping down as chairman.
- US is much less dependent on oil since the 1970's, which means the current impacts are also less. If this goes on for longer and prices go much higher, then we will feel the effect more. 
- When gas prices go up, people will spend less on other things which hill hit GDP. So spending going down offsets inflation, just have to see to what degree.
- Datacenter demand and buildout is big contributor to economic growth as well as resilient consumer spending.
- His last words were "I won't see you next time"

*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/bnc9/fomc-notes-april-2026*

### Post #965: 2026-06-09 01:34 `[Dd]`

**Historical Market Performance following a ~2.4% or more Market Drop**

I performed a study over the last 27 years (1999 - 2026) of all the times the S&P500 Index posted a Daily Candle of ~2.4% or more Above the 50 SMA and near the ATH (which closely replicates what we saw on 6/5/26).

These are the results:
- There were a total of 14 instances that fit this criteria
- Of those 14, 2 of them were similar to 6/5/26 (see *footnote on below table)
- The 2 similar instances show a recovery of that candle within 2 weeks to 1 month
- Of all 14 instances, the candle tends to show the bottom of the pullback with price recovering the within 2 weeks
- On average, the market stays flat on the day after the drop

*As an investor and trader, it's not your job to predict what the markets will do - but rather prepare for any likely scenario to happen so you can take advantage of the opportunities presented to you.*

*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/bqsA/historical-market-performance-*

Very important FOMC meeting today with the new Fed Chairman, Kevin Warsh, who will give us a lot of insight (hopefully) into the direction he wants to take with inflation and interest rates moving forward. 

Some background on Warsh: 
- He historically prefers to reduce the Fed's balance sheet as it distorts markets and complicates short-term rate policy
- He envisions the Fed only uses its balance sheet in times of market stress and not a regular tool for influencing financial conditions
- He is historically hawkish and is skeptic of quantitative easing (known from his record from the 2008 crisis)

It's very interesting to see someone with a hawkish background being appointed by Trump who is dovish and wants to cut interest rates. Has Warsh's ideas changed over the years? Is it true that he will use a new way to measure inflation? Hopefully we get answers to these today.

### Post #975: 2026-06-17 19:18

**FOMC Notes June 2026 | First Warsh Meeting**

- "Good Day" lol.
- Price stability and max employment remains as dual mandate.
- Rates held the same.
- Members of the FOMC are unanimous that this committee will deliver price stability.
- Shorter and simpler policy statement, only facts with absence of forward guidance.
- Warsh will refrain with giving projections.
- Warsh emphasized desire to re-assess and reform current Fed practices, using task forces.

__Q&A:__
- Inflation is a choice - this committee chooses to deliver 2.0% inflation.
- Monthly press conferences may not continue unless there is something substantial to communicate.
- Most data sources come with old-fashioned survey methods with less applicable prompts. Warsh wants to minimize revisions and is open to new data sources and analytical techniques to refine the data they use.
- The idea of not providing forward guidance is to have financial markets react to the data itself instead of the Fed. That will also provide the Fed with more data that reflect on what markets are thinking.
- Why not raise rates today based on the statement and inflation risks mentioned? Warsh didn't give any answer and said refer to the statement.
- Can productivity lower interest rates? (AI) - Over the long-term, US is a winner as we go down this path. Didn't answer how it will affect rates coming down.
- Warsh doesn't believe that you have to choose between growth and inflation. He believes it's the Fed's job to make inflation 2% without it affecting growth and employment.
- AI adding more to demand or supply? AI is adding to demand side and GDP for sure. There's a race between supply and demand, nut supply side is more uncertain and inferred.
- 50% of the committee think rates should be same or lower, other 50% think they should be higher.
- Jobs data is trending in a positive direction. 

*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/brgt/fomc-notes-june-2026-or-first-*

### Post #1006: 2026-07-29 19:16

**FOMC Notes July 2026 | Rates Held Steady**


- 9-3 vote to maintain current rates.
- Economy shows resilience and growth despite shocks.
- Committee is steering clear of forecasts - they want to market to be "players" and not "refs".
- Fed reiterates that there is no soft inflation target - only one target of 2%.
- Nominal and real yields are materially higher across the treasury curve - the most significant in the last 2 decades.
- Strong growth of tech capex is remarkable, but doesn't make the Fed's role easier. Forward growth rates of 20%, which helps sustain the momentum of manufacturing.

__Q&A:__ 
- What message are you getting from the markets on where policy should be? The message has been unfiltered and direct, which is what the Feds intended from the lack of forward guidance. Message (from treasury market) is: economic output is solid and steady.
- What arguments did the 3 dissents have? Robust discussion about what we can do and what we want to do. Nothing inertial - lots of agreement on the hard questions.
- Why should rates not be higher today? Rates are higher today than they were 42 days ago because the treasury market has raised long-term rates. Monetary policy matters by how the real economy is affected. 
- Is rising rates the best remedy to bring inflation down if it stays elevated? Rates are part of the solution, but not an isolated one. Ensuring expectations are set on the same 2% target also matters, which is why Fed statements have been emphasizing this.
- Fed takes shocks seriously, and there have been a number of them. Fed is trying to understand the impact of these shocks and they make this job tougher. 
- Warsh doesn't believe price stability and full employment is a trade-off. Instead, if and when we deliver, it will satisfy both mandates.

Personal note: Warsh gives very political answers, which I'm not a fan of.

*Link: https://afterhour.com/MarketVictor/btmr/fomc-notes-july-2026-or-rates-*
